## 2026-10-31

- **feat(lofts): a storey's own deck size now decides who earns a second zombie — the named "one per upper floor regardless of size" gap, closed.** `tools/build_loft_city.py` asked for population per **floor** and never per **deck**, so a full 254×68 pair deck and the 254×34 stepped-back storey above it, in the same building, each got exactly one body. `population_plan` now receives the levels, measures every storey's own deck through the same predicate the placement loop already uses (`storey_deck_areas`), staffs every upper floor once exactly as before, and then deals the spare slots out **one building at a time, with the deck picking which floor inside each**, until `NPC_BUDGET` (69 — the engine's own simultaneous ceiling, `MAX_CLIENTS` 70 minus the human) is spent. The city goes from **61 authored characters to 69**: eight slots of squad budget were sitting unused, one of them now on every loft. **The first cut was wrong and the distribution caught it:** every full pair deck measures the same 21964, so a plain global area sort handed all eight slots to `loft_a` and `loft_b` on the name tiebreak — the exact "building one packed while building eight is empty" failure this file's own even-spread pass exists to prevent. Hence: size decides the floor, the round-robin decides the building. The rule is asserted, not just the total — at most two bodies per floor, at least one upper floor earns its second, the spare slots are spread across buildings, and each extra sits on the biggest deck of its OWN building. **The first version of that assertion failed against its own rule** by counting ground floors (two by design, not by area) as deck-earned; the comment records it, because a check that compares the wrong set is exactly the kind that passes vacuously. Verified: `python3 tools/build_loft_city.py --check` green (the theme/geometry/chest/EMPIRE-ground checks unchanged), and the written city re-read at **69 characters, 1739 walls, 45 staffed upper floors, 8 of them doubled**. NOT verified, named: no window on this box, so the extra bodies are verified as authored data and a clean parse, never watched fighting; a doubled floor shares that floor's four corner spots, so even a 254×68 deck fields at most two; and the population is still static — no wave spawner reads this plan yet.

- **fix(noobtopia): the ambient seed mixer was two different generators pretending to be one — MINSTD on both sides now, pinned by both test suites.** The C host mixed seeds with `(seed * 1103515245 + 12345) & 0x7FFFFFFF` while `NOOBTOPIA/src/ambient.lua` ran the same arithmetic in Lua numbers, which in Luau are IEEE doubles — exact only to 2^53 — so above a seed near 2^31 the low bits were rounded away and the two worlds picked different lines from the same seed (seed 2147483646: `2087949151` in C, `2087949312` through doubles). Nothing could notice: SHANKPIT only ever ran the C, and the Luau was reviewed but never executed until Lua 5.4 landed on this box. Both sides are now MINSTD (`* 48271 mod 2^31-1`, peak ~1e14, exact in both), done in `int64` so the C multiply cannot wrap first. The fix does change every existing ambient draw — that is correct, because the old "stream" was two streams wearing one name. Verified: `make test-noobtopia` green end to end (ambient, walk, arena, ASan+UBSan) with four mixer vectors pinned in `ambient_life_test.c` — 0, 42, 999999 and **2147483646**, the seed that used to diverge — and the identical four asserted by `NOOBTOPIA/tests/ambient_test.lua`; `lua tests/ambient_test.lua` and `lua tests/walk_test.lua` both clean. NOT verified, named: Roblox still has no Studio or place here, so the double-precision path is proven by arithmetic and forced-double simulation rather than by running Luau itself.

- **feat(noobtopia): where an NPC drifts is now a shared decision — `walk_rules.h` host, `make test-walk-rules`, and the parity fixture both worlds assert.** SHANKPIT's NPCs could not move as part of the merge because nothing decided where, and NOOBTOPIA's noobs wandered on a 33 s timer nobody shared. `packages/simulation/walk_rules.h` is the thin host: it holds a position and a permille credit and asks PARENA (`stdlib/noobtopia/walk_rules.prn`, emitted by `scripts/gen_noobtopia_rules.sh`) for every decision, integrating with the **same integer algorithm** `NOOBTOPIA/src/wander.lua` runs — one whole unit per tick, a target rejected if it leaves the agent's leash with all 8 headings tried — so a seeded NPC walks the same path in both worlds and neither can move half a unit. **The test caught a real design error in the host's own contract:** `tick()` first returned "is mid-leg", so a STARTLED noob (0 permille) reported walking every tick while its position never changed; it now returns "stepped this tick" and the leg flag is separate. The Luau test failed the identical assertion at the same moment, which is the parity machinery doing its job rather than two suites that happen to agree. Verified: `make test-walk-rules` (**13 parity vectors + the compass, mood, leash, termination and determinism properties, 0 failed**, ASan+UBSan), `make test-noobtopia` green end to end (ambient, walk, arena — the arena suite unchanged), `lua tests/walk_test.lua` and `lua tests/ambient_test.lua` clean on Lua 5.4. NOT verified, named: the lobby is still not built on this box (no SDL2 headers), so `walk_rules.h` is proven headless and **not wired into the lobby's NPC draw loop yet** — same standing limit as the ambient slice.

- **fix(nock): the NOCK tile now really starts a server, says why when it cannot, and everything it says lands in a text file instead of nowhere.** Founder: *"can we fix the local nock? i click it in shankpit apps and nothing happens ensure it is bundled into the client and add a separate local nock bat start file or whatever and ensure any logs are output into a text file not just swallowed when a screen opens and closes right away"*. Two real causes, both structural. **(1) There was never a Windows server to start, let alone one to bundle.** The standalone NOCK server is `tools/local_nock.prn`, a PARENA program over `net/tcp` and `process/run-capture` — and PARENA's own runtime guarded both `#if !defined(_WIN32)`, with a comment saying so outright ("genuinely absent on Windows, not stubbed"). So `bundled/dw_nock.exe`, the exact path the APPS tile looks for, had never been buildable; the tile could only ever answer "server not found". PARENA's runtime now carries that host layer for real: winsock `tcp_connect/tcp_listen/tcp_accept/tcp_read/tcp_write/tcp_close` with the same contract as the POSIX block (stop at the Content-Length body, or at EOF when there is none, with the same 30s idle-read net), `_popen`/`_pclose` for `run-capture` (the `git add`/`git commit` a level save performs), and `CreateProcessA`/`TerminateProcess` for `process-spawn`/`process-kill` — the last two only because importing `(process)` emits them and the Windows link genuinely needs them, which the build found rather than guessed. `tools/local_nock_host.c` grew the single platform branch it needed (FindFirstFileA listing behind a shared `ln_dir_names`, `MoveFileExA` replace-on-rename, `_getpid`), and `make local-nock-win` cross-compiles the whole thing. **(2) Every decision the launch made was thrown away.** The server is spawned detached, so on Windows its stdout/stderr had nowhere to go at all (`CreateProcessA` with `bInheritHandles=FALSE` and a zeroed `STARTUPINFOA`) — precisely where "levels/ missing", "port taken" or "no such binary" would have been written. `lobby_launch_nock` now appends every step to `<root>/var/logs/nock.log`, redirects the child's own stdout+stderr into that same file (inheritable share-permitted handle, with the parent's own handle opened `_fsopen(..., _SH_DENYNO)` because a plain `fopen` would deny the child's writer), sets the child's cwd to the repo root (the `levels/` path and the git commit on save are both relative to it), and every failure it prints on screen names that log. **The separate start file** is `local_nock.bat`: bundled → `bin` → Python fallback, all output appended to `var\logs\nock.log`, the editor opened once the port has had a moment, and on exit the window prints the log's last lines and waits instead of closing on the error a user could not read. It ships in the client zip next to `PLAY.bat`, and the release now builds `bundled/dw_nock.exe` plus `bundled/levels/` + `bundled/tools/nock_editor.html` (the root a packaged client resolves), on Linux too. Verified: `make bin/dw_nock.exe` produces a real `PE32+ executable (console) x86-64, for MS Windows` (378,885 bytes) with this box's mingw toolchain; `make bin/local_nock_parena` still builds and `python3 tools/test_local_nock.py --binary ./bin/local_nock_parena` reports **16 checks, 0 failed** after the host changes; the lobby `main.c` passes `gcc -fsyntax-only`; `release.yml` parses as YAML. NOT verified, named: **the .exe was never run** — there is no wine here — so the winsock path is compiled and linked, not exercised, and Win64's 64-bit `SOCKET` is narrowed to the `I32` the stdlib passes around (safe for real socket handles, but a narrowing rather than a guarantee); `spawn_detached_impl`/`process_kill_impl` are implemented but nothing in NOCK calls them; and no browser tab was opened from a real click, because the lobby cannot link on this box (`../EDITOR.GAME` is absent) — the tile's own path is verified as a compile plus an audit of what it now logs, not as a click. The PARENA half of this lives in that repo's own runtime and needs a push there for CI to see it.

- **fix(render): the black frame — three vehicle draw paths left `GL_LIGHTING` on for everything drawn after them.** Founder: *"we just have some weird bug where everything is black now like the vehicles totally black the screen text totally black even the background for the log text on the screen totally black"*. Nothing to do with the new lighting: `draw_drone_entity`, `draw_airplane_model` and the rocket-car branch of `draw_buggy_entity` each opened with `glDisable(GL_LIGHTING)` and closed with a flat `glEnable(GL_LIGHTING)`, so from the first plane/drone/rocket car onward the rest of the frame was **lit with no light source enabled at all** (none of those paths enable `GL_LIGHT0`) — material ambient 0.2 times light-model ambient 0.2, about 4% brightness, i.e. black — and because nothing in the client ever enabled `GL_COLOR_MATERIAL`, `glColor` was dead in that state too, so the turtle-glyph HUD text, the log panel background and every prop that colours itself with `glColor` lost their colour in the same pass. The bridge now spawns every vehicle including the C-110, so this fired on every frame of the founder's own default map. All three save and **restore** the state via `glIsEnabled`, the same discipline `retro_sky.c` already uses, and `draw_widget_affordances` restores lighting/texture instead of forcing both on. The buggy was also black by construction, separately: the whole model is authored with `glColor` (body, wheels, accents) while the material carried 0.06 ambient / 0.14 diffuse, so the car rendered from that flat dark material instead of its own colours — it now enables `GL_COLOR_MATERIAL` with `GL_AMBIENT_AND_DIFFUSE` inside its own existing `glPushAttrib`, gets a sky fill through `GL_LIGHT_MODEL_AMBIENT` (the same physical reason `retro_lighting.c`'s SUN_SKY_FILL exists, so a shaded flank never falls to black), and the body/wheel colours that were written while `glColor` was ignored are lifted from 0.06/0.10 to graphite 0.24/0.16 so the car is a car and not a hole in the road. Verified: `gcc -fsyntax-only` over the whole lobby `main.c` clean, and an audit of every `GL_LIGHTING` site in it — each enable is now either inside a `glPushAttrib`/`glPopAttrib` or guarded by a saved flag. NOT verified, named: `make lobby` still cannot link on this box (`../EDITOR.GAME` is not checked out), so this is a compile and a state audit, **not a run** — no screenshot of the fixed frame, and the brightness figures above are the GL state machine's own arithmetic, not a measured pixel.

- **feat(lofts): the lofts are phi-cubed landmarks, and the pre-phi city is preserved as its own reachable map.** Founder: *"the new lofts are tiny ... scale up by double using phi"*, then *"scale the lofts again, to phi cubed, and re-place and re-bake them"* and *"restore the pre-scale loft city as its own map file and reachable level, keeping the big-loft one as the default"*. `LOFT_SCALE` is now **phi³ = 4.236** (was phi² = 2.618), one constant applied to the FLATTENED building so the authored tree stays at human size: a pair is **254x85** and a storey **34** (measured: `loft_a` widest box 60 -> 254.2, tallest wall top 44 -> 186.4). Deeper than "double", which is the point -- at phi² a 157-wide building in a 333-wide world was still furniture next to the city's own blocks. **Re-placed**: `--replace-placements` clears the frozen spots and the chooser runs against the new footprint, which is required after any scale change and is now named in the builder's own comment. **Re-baked**: `tools/update_baked_level.py --id 92` refreshes the zombies default, so the map the sandbox actually loads carries the big lofts. **The fork is preserved, not overwritten**: the pre-phi city (loft_a 60 wide) is restored from `8c2866a` to **`var/maps/nextown_prephi.json`** -- the local map directory the client's own level picker and `--level` reach -- renamed to `nextown_prephi` for the browser, so the small-loft world is playable as its own level while the big-loft one stays the zombies default. Verified: `python3 tools/build_loft_city.py --replace-placements` writes 1739 walls across all eight lofts with the phi³ extents above, `--check` green, `make test-loft-city` **20 checks, 0 failed** (the authored ground, the chest that breaks, all 61 characters, and a top-floor zombie walking on the new 34-unit decks), `make test-baked-levels` green (64 main levels + 1 zombies default, all export). NOT verified, named: no window here, so the size is verified as geometry and a clean parse, not seen; the phi³ placement is tight (loft_h reaches z = -1091, outside the level's own declared 300 depth) and the two worlds share one `levels/lofts/placements.json`, which currently holds the BIG placements -- rebuilding the small city again would need its own placements pass.

- **feat(anim): an imported skin now plays all thirteen states — and the clip's own manifest, not a copied table, says which ones loop.** Nine of the thirteen contract clips were loaded, contract-named and unreachable: the kit loader only ever kept idle/walk/greet/dance through `GSeq`, so `anim_clipset.h` could pick a state and nothing could draw it. `GbandSkelNpcKit` now carries the thirteen (`load_state_clips`, loaded by `gband_skel_npc_load_clipset` after the four a body needs), and the new `gband_skel_npc_draw_state` samples the state it is handed, re-initialising that slot's player on a state or kit change. `main.c`'s mannequin path calls it with `anim_state_for_player(p, 0, 0)`, so a player's own imported body runs, crouch-walks, jumps, is in the air, fires, reloads, swings, uses an ability and climbs; every built-in character is still a 4-clip kit and keeps the old path untouched. The loop flag is read from **each clip's own `.gband.json`** (the binder fills it from `anim_rules.prn`) rather than copied into the renderer, which is what makes a fire/reload hold its last tick instead of snapping to frame 0. The two clip-name tables — `anim_state.h`'s and the kit's — are separate by design (`packages/goldenband` does not depend on `packages/simulation`), so a test pins them. Verified: `make test-anim-states` — **11 checks, 0 failed** on the committed Mike bundle: the two tables agree string for string across all thirteen, 13/13 states load, idle and run sample different vertices, fire holds across a 5-second advance, an out-of-range state still draws (resolving to idle), and a kit with no states resolves to −1 so the caller keeps the old path. NOT verified, named: no window here, so the poses were compared as skinned vertex data through the draw callback, never seen on screen; the state is chosen per PLAYER, so an NPC being greeted still uses its `anim_override` wave rather than the state machine; and a built-in cosmetic still has no thirteen-clip bundle to play.

- **feat(stats): a GFD job now changes a live player — and applying the bridge found that haste was inverted.** Phase 4 shipped the table and the mapping; nothing read them, so a player was 100 HP and the stock ROF whatever job a save said. New `packages/simulation/stats_apply.h` holds a per-slot job/level roster (**no `PlayerState` field**: 0 is WAR, so every zeroed struct would have silently become a Warrior) and installs two hooks into `packages/common/physics.h` — the same pattern `phys_set_map_damage_hooks` already uses, so a host that never calls `stats_apply_install()` is exactly the old engine. `phys_respawn` now grants the job's health through `g_phys_stats_health_hook`, and the fire path's `attack_cooldown = WPN_STATS[w].rof` goes through `g_phys_stats_rof_hook`. Reachable with `--job NIN:75` on the lobby (`stats_apply_set(0, …)`) and on the server (`stats_apply_set_all`), with the parser refusing `nin`, `QQQ:5`, `NIN:` and `NIN:7x` instead of quietly handing out a Warrior. **The real find**: `sb_cooldown` multiplied the ROF by haste, so a higher-AGI job (higher haste permille) shot *slower* — the exact opposite of its own comment, invisible for months because no call site read it. Fixed at the source (`stats_bridge.prn`), regenerated with the real PARENA bootstrap into `stats_bridge.c`, mirrored in `stats_bridge.h`, and the two bridge assertions that had encoded the inversion are corrected with a comment saying so: a 1300-haste job turns a 20-tick knife into 15 ticks, 700 into 28. Verified: `make test-stats-apply` — **22 checks, 0 failed** through the REAL `phys_respawn` and the real cooldown expression (jobless slot is unchanged at 100 HP/stock ROF, a job changes both, a fast job is never slower than a slow one, no weapon reaches zero ticks, other slots untouched, the parser's refusals); `make test-stats-bridge` — **41 checks, 0 failed** after the regeneration (generated == header across all 23 jobs × levels −2..80 and cooldown across ROF 0..60 × haste 600..1400); `make -B server` links; the lobby `main.c` compiles clean (`-fsyntax-only`). NOT verified, named: movement and a damage scalar are exposed but **no call site multiplies by them yet**, crit/evade never touch the hit path, nothing persists a job across launches, and no windowed match was played with a job — the health and cooldown are proven at the authority seam, not watched in a session.

- **feat(lofts): the city's population fits the engine, its ground can be taken, and NOCK can place the things it is made of — the named gaps from the loft pass, closed.** Working back through what the loft-city work said it had not done, three of those were real defects rather than missing polish. **The population did not fit.** The city authored **73** characters against **69** simultaneous slots (`MAX_CLIENTS` 70 minus the human); the loader kept all 73 and the brains could only ever place 69, so four were authored, saved and never existed. `tools/build_loft_city.py` now has `NPC_BUDGET = 69` and a `population_plan` that staffs every ground floor first (two each — what a player meets walking in) and then spreads the upper floors **evenly** across the whole city — the i-th of `keep` floors taken from the `i*len/keep`-th candidate — instead of packing building one and leaving building eight empty. Worse, the file was carrying **12 phantom zombies**: a strip that removes only the entries the last run *recorded* cannot remove entries an older format wrote (`role`+`x/y/z`, no EMPIRE fields at all), so they had become permanent. Elimination is now by shape (a nameless role-7 body with every other field at its default is this tool's own output) as well as by record, and `check()` asserts the array **the engine loads**, not the tool's own record of it — those two came apart, and only a check on the file can catch it. **The city had no EMPIRE ground**, so `gather` and `claim` fell back to `story_ai`'s default ring at the world origin, several hundred metres from any loft: units with orders and nothing in reach. Each loft now authors one resource node outside its front door (amount scaled by its theme's own loot table) and one claim cell on the ground floor (radius 10). **NOCK could not place what a level is made of** — the two tools it lacked are now real: **Object** (`O`) places a level reference (`ref_level_id`, height, quarter-turns) which is what a floor, a pair and a building all are, and **Node**/**Claim** (`N`/`C`) place EMPIRE ground with an amount and a radius; **Erase** removes all three, and all three are drawn (references as green anchors, nodes as diamonds, claim cells as rings at their **real** radius so two overlapping cells look overlapping). The named "edit the sub levels and the references all update" affordance now exists for the composed tree instead of only for its JSON. Verified: `python3 tools/build_loft_city.py --check` (the new plan, the per-loft ground, and the file-vs-record assertions all green) and the city written to **1739 walls / 61 characters / 24 chests / 8 nodes / 8 cells**; a new `make test-loft-city` — **20 checks, 0 failed** — drives the *file* through the engine's own loaders: 24 chests placed into the real pool, a rare one **shot apart until it drops a gun**, all 61 characters spawned through `story_ai_spawn_unit` (no silent slot exhaustion), the highest-floor zombie ticked for sixty frames until it **closes distance on the player** without changing floor, and the city's authored ground loaded through `story_ai_empire_load_level` (proving it is out at a loft rather than at the origin). The editor's JavaScript passes `node --check`. NOT verified, named: no window here, so nothing new was *seen* — the population, the markers and the walk are asserted through the C loaders, not watched; the node's claim radius is one per loft rather than per-theme; the 12 phantom characters are gone but the reason they existed (an older writer, no longer present) was not reproduced, only made harmless; and `make lobby` still cannot link on this box (`../EDITOR.GAME` is not checked out), so the client half of this pass is the editor's own file, not a run of the client.

- **fix(release): the Windows OS-apps step can no longer hang the whole build on a Go module download.** Founder: *"the shankpit os build is like hanging on the shankpit apps ... go: downloading github.com/veandco/go-sdl2 v0.4.38 ..."*. `go build` has no timeout of its own, and PITVIPER/IDUNA.GAME are sibling repos whose modules are **not vendored** in this tree, so a slow or unreachable module proxy parked the job indefinitely and every artifact waited on it. Three changes, each addressing a real part of that: an **`actions/cache`** step for `~/go/pkg/mod` + `~/.cache/go-build` keyed on `os_apps/**/go.sum` (so release N+1 does not re-download go-sdl2/x-image/x-sys/x-crypto unless a dependency actually changed); **`GOTOOLCHAIN=local`** so Go cannot also decide to download a whole toolchain; and a `go_os_app` helper that bounds `go mod download` at **240s** and the build at **420s**, and if a module still cannot be fetched it prints a loud line, **skips that one app and lets the release continue** — the lobby's APPS button already reports an unbundled app honestly, and a network hiccup must not hold up the client, server and world that live in this repo. Verified so far: the workflow parses as YAML and the extracted step passes `bash -n`; `grep` confirms nothing later in the pipeline asserts `bundled/pitviper.exe` or `bundled/idunagame.exe` exist, so a skipped app cannot turn into a false release failure. NOT verified, named: **there is no Go toolchain on the box this was written on**, so neither the success nor the failure path of `go_os_app` was executed — the skip path is reasoned about (`if go_os_app ...` guard, `timeout ... || exit 1` inside the subshell, `set -e` interaction) but not run, and with no Windows cgo SDK a real PITVIPER cross-build was not produced either. The next real CI run is the proof this fix needs.

- **feat(lofts): every floor is a fight, every building has its own loot, and the composed tree is now editable in NOCK.** Founder: *"populate upper floors; peer theme loot; edit ineriors in nock"*. **Upper floors**: each storey now authors its own zombie, and the positions are taken from that storey's OWN deck box rather than the building's overall footprint — without that, a stepped-back building (loft_c/d/g) would put its upper-floor population in the air where the lower stack used to be. Two zombies on the ground floor, one on each floor above: 61 loft zombies, 73 authored characters in the city (the 12 bridge ones included), which needed `LEVEL_BOXES_MAX_CHARACTERS` and `STORY_AI_MAX` to move from 64 to **128** together. **Per-theme loot**: chest tiers now belong to the theme (`THEME_LOOT`) instead of a 0,1,2 rotation repeated eight times — a lab is `2,2,1` (rare), a warehouse `0,0,1`, a clinic `1,2,2`, a cafe `0,0,1` — and the chests are spread up the building instead of all sitting on the ground floor. **NOCK**: `tools/local_nock.py`'s LevelStore now lists and saves one directory deep (`lofts/loft_props_cafe`), `validate_level` accepts a **composition** level (`objects` with `ref_level_id`, quarter-turn `rot_y` only) as well as a `walls` level, names are slug-checked per segment and re-checked for containment after joining, and a new `POST /api/lofts/rebuild` runs the same two commands a human would (`build_loft_city.py` then `update_baked_level.py`) and commits the flattened city + refreshed baked default — the editor gets a **Rebuild lofts → city** button for it, and draws a composition level's references as dashed anchors with their target ids. The editor no longer injects materials or a spawner into a composed level on save, which would have rewritten a floor into a different kind of level. Verified: `make test-lofts` (storey-aware population and per-theme loot counts asserted, themes' loot tables must be distinct, caps checked), `./bin/leveltool check` — **73 characters**, 1739 walls, 24 chests, and a live local-NOCK run: the nested tree lists, a composition level saves, a props module saves, `../x` / `lofts/../x` / `a/b/c` are all refused, and the rebuild endpoint returns the builder's own output; plus `make test-local-nock` (16/16, the flat-level and session contracts untouched), `make test-level-save`, `make test-baked-levels`, `make test-witness-ai-zombies`, `make test-empire-spawn`, and `make -B server`. NOT done, named: no window, so a zombie on a loft floor has still never been watched pathing; upper floors use one zombie each regardless of floor size; and the NOCK editor has no object *placement* tool — a composed level's references can be seen and its JSON saved, but adding a reference still means editing the file or the builder's blueprint.

- **feat(levels): the eight lofts now have eight different interiors — the shell is shared, the rooms are not.** Founder: *"interior detail pass"*. The furniture that was baked into the bay primitives (so every building was identical inside) moved out into **themed props modules**, one per building, authored inside a single 20x20 bay: `loft_props_office` (desks, filing run, partition), `cafe` (counter, machine, tables, menu board), `lounge` (two couches, coffee table, two floor lamps), `workshop` (workbench, tool rack, crates), `lab` (benches, fume hood, glass sample tank), `shop` (two shelving runs, till, window sign), `clinic` (two beds, cabinet, glass screen), `warehouse` (pallets, a stacked pallet, racking). Each variant declares a `theme` and places that theme's props **on both floors of every pair it places**, in the glass bay, plus one on the lobby floor — so a building's interior repeats up it exactly the way the shell does, and editing one props module restyles only the building that owns it. Nothing is placed in the core bay, which is where the stair well is. The bays keep their own fixtures (a light each, the crates by the stair). Net cost is small because the generic furniture paid for most of it: 1714 -> 1739 walls, still inside `LEVEL_BOXES_MAX` 2048 (with the builder's own warning now live at 1600, so the next thing added here is told to trim rather than discovering the cap on load). Verified: `make test-lofts` — the eight themes are owned one each (a duplicate is an error), every variant references **its own** props module and no other building's, the existing checks (distinct buildings, stairs, 16 zombies, 24 chests, idempotent flatten, caps) all still hold; plus `./bin/leveltool check` round-trip, `make test-level-save`, `make test-baked-levels`, `make -B server`, and the live baked level 92 refreshed to the new city. NOT done, named: still nothing seen on screen — the interiors are verified as authored geometry and a clean parse, not as rooms that look right; the props are the same on every storey of a building (the theme repeats, it does not thin out upstairs); and the upper floors' props are not yet populated with anything that fights.

- **feat(levels): the lofts are furnished, lit, populated and looted — and a level can author its own chests and its own crowd.** Founder: *"dress interiors; populate lofts"*. **Furniture** lives in the bay primitives, so it repeats up every storey of every building for free: the glass bay gets a reception desk, a shelf run and an HPS fixture; the open bay a table, a couch and a floor lamp; the stair core two stacked crates kept clear of the well (nothing is placed inside `x -4..4, z -10..2`, the opening the ramp climbs through). That is **+8 boxes per floor** — 1714 walls for the whole city, still comfortably inside `LEVEL_BOXES_MAX` 2048. **Population**: each building authors two zombies on its ground floor (archetype `EU_ARCH_ZOMBIE`, role 7 pursuer, faction/orders left -1 so the archetype's own values win), placed on footprint fractions that avoid the stair core. That needed a real cap change — `LEVEL_BOXES_MAX_CHARACTERS` and `STORY_AI_MAX` were both **16**, the story campaign's cast size, which would have allowed four zombies in a city of eight buildings; both are now **64** and the tip's own comment says they must move together. **Loot**: the level schema gained `chests` (`tier`, `x`, `y`, `z`) — parsed and re-serialized by `level_boxes.h` with the same small scanner as every other array, capped at `CHEST_MAX` 32 with the tier clamped to `chests.h`'s own 0/1/2 — and `lobby_apply_story_level` places authored chests through `chests_place`, resetting the pool and installing the hit hooks only when a level actually authored any, so survival's own road-ring seed is untouched and every level without the key behaves exactly as before. Each building authors three chests (wood/reinforced/rare), 24 in the city. Verified: `make test-lofts` (8 buildings, 1714 walls, distinct, stairs present, idempotent, and now asserts 16 zombies + 24 chests against the engine's own caps), `./bin/leveltool check` round-trips the city including the new `chests` array, `make test-level-save` (the save/load contract that would otherwise drop a new array), `make test-baked-levels`, and `make -B server` links. NOT done, named: nothing was seen on screen, so "furnished" is verified as authored geometry rather than as a room that looks good; the chests are placed and hooked but this pass did not drive a shot into one; and the population is two per building on the ground floor rather than a per-storey spread.

- **fix(build): a checkout without the sibling `../PARENA` builds again — a generated file's regeneration rule was a hard build break.** `make` failed with `No rule to make target '../PARENA/stdlib/shankpit/lighting_rules.prn', needed by 'packages/simulation/lighting_rules.c'` on any machine that has the game repo but not the sibling engine checkout. The generated `.c` files are **committed**, so regeneration is an explicit `make regen-*` job, not a build prerequisite: the four rules in `mk/brick.mk` (ocean, noobtopia's two, structure, lighting) are now declared only when their `.prn`/data source actually exists (`$(wildcard ...)`), and a missing sibling simply means the committed output is used. Verified by dry-running the server build with the source pointed at a path that does not exist — `make -n server LIGHTING_RULES_PRN=/definitely/missing.prn` exits 0 with no `No rule to make target`, and `make -B server` links.

- **feat(levels): the lofts are now a recursive level tree, and eight of them stand in the zombies city — different heights, stepped-back towers, all carveable.** Founder: *"take the primatives of the city that already build the city these are recursive levels ... the lofts building is made up of floors that are actually 2 floors that are actually built out of 3 levels that are actually each 2 levels ish ... make variations and then throw the combinations together to make like 8 unique lofts with different numbers of floors etc and add them to the default zombies map ... so we can edit the sub levels and the refrerences all update"*. The model is IDUNA's own, not a new one: a level owns `walls` and `objects` (`ref_level_id`, `x`, `y`, `z`, `rot_y`), and composition is recursive. Because the native client only ever loads ONE flat level (IDUNA flattens server-side), the tree lives in `levels/lofts/` as real level JSON — `loft_bay_glass` / `loft_bay_core` / `loft_bay_open` are the primitives; `loft_floor` places those three side by side; `loft_pair` stacks **two floors** (the founder's "floors that are actually 2 floors"); `loft_lobby` and `loft_roof` cap them; `loft_a`..`loft_h` place 2–5 pairs each, some stepped back — and `tools/build_loft_city.py` (`make build-lofts`) flattens them into `var/zombie/nextown_zombies.json` exactly the way `flattenObjects` does, including the Y-only 0/90/180/270 rotation that swaps `sx`/`sz` and never rotates height, material and name carry-through, cycle detection and a depth cap. Editing a bay therefore changes every building that references it, because no variant duplicates module geometry — only references it. **Placement** is chosen once from the real city (a grid scan that rejects anything touching existing geometry in the 0–70 height band, so buildings are never dropped on the bridge deck, through a road slab or on the spawn), nearest-first from the spawn with a 220-unit gap, written to `levels/lofts/placements.json` and frozen so later rebuilds are byte-identical. **Destructibility is by construction, not by tuning**: partitions and glass are the thin boxes and floors/ceilings are slabs, which is exactly the split `brick_world_box_is_slab` already makes, so a carved room keeps a floor to stand on; the new `glass` material is NOCK's own glass entry (same `shader_name`, same texture), added to the city's material table because nextown had never carried it. The export is idempotent against **two** markers: the `loft_*` wall names *and* the exact geometry recorded in the level's own `loft_city` key — necessary because `LevelBox` has no name field at all, so a city re-saved from EDIT MAP loses the names and a rebuild would otherwise stack a second copy of all eight buildings. Verified: `make test-lofts` — every reference resolves, no cycles, the eight variants are proven **distinct** (no two flatten to the same wall set), each has carveable geometry, and flattening is idempotent; two consecutive builds produce a byte-identical city (same sha1); `./bin/leveltool check` round-trips 1234 walls, 12 characters, 1 exit; and a C harness over the real `level_boxes_load_from_file` reports **1234 walls, glass resolving to material 7, 504 brick-fracture parents, 272 slabs, 458 static** — all inside `LEVEL_BOXES_MAX`/`BF_MAX_PARENTS` 2048. **Stairs, and the live map**: the core now cuts a real stair well — deck and ceiling are each three pieces around an 8x12 opening and a `ramp: true` wedge climbs through it from one floor's deck top to the next, so a building is a stairwell with floors on it rather than a stack of sealed slabs (48 ramps across the eight, 38 of them carrying a per-box `rot_y` because a wedge cannot be rotated by swapping extents the way a cube can — `transform_wall` keeps ramps on the engine's obb path and pre-rotates everything else into plain carveable AABBs). The **live** zombies default is refreshed too: `packages/world/baked_levels_gen.h` id 92 now carries this city (494 -> 1330 walls) via the new `tools/update_baked_level.py`, which patches one export and its registry counts rather than re-baking all 65 levels from a server, and `make test-baked-levels` still reports 64 main levels + 1 zombies default all exporting. NOT done, named: **the authored tree is not yet editable in NOCK** — `tools/local_nock.py`'s LevelStore is flat (`levels/*.json`) and `validate_level` expects `walls`, so sub-levels and `objects` show up only as files; the editor affordance (recursive listing, objects panel, flatten-on-export) is the remaining piece, and IDUNA still owns the server-side flatten it already implements. Nothing was screenshotted (no GL/display here), so the eight buildings are verified as geometry and as borne-out parse, not as seen in the world.

- **feat(levels): the zombies bridge is a motor pool — every vehicle spawns on its deck, and the C-110 finally sits on level geometry instead of the terrain.** Founder: *"add spawners for all the vehicles on top of the bridge on the zombies level ... but the plane needs to be actually on the bridge so it can take off"*. `var/zombie/nextown_zombies.json` gained a `vehicle_spawns` row on the spawn deck (`z ≈ −228`, deck top `12.556`): buggy, rocket car, heli, recon drone, mini fly, then the C-110 further along so the whole bridge is ahead of it as runway — all nose along +X (`yaw −90`, the same axis the flight test takes off on). The C-110 needed a real fix to be there at all: `af_ground_y` was terrain + taxi clearance, so a plane authored on a bridge spawned **under** it and fell away from the map, because terrain was the only surface the flight model knew. It now asks the active geometry for a floor (`af_level_floor`, the same `map_geo` boxes `heli_point_collides` already tests, skipping boxes a brick fracture has hidden), spawns on the highest surface under the gear, and clamps per tick only to surfaces at or below the hull — so a plane flying over a building, or under a bridge, is not snapped onto it. Verified: `make test-airplane-flight` — a new case points `map_geo` at a real deck Box and asserts the C-110 spawns at deck + clearance and is still resting there after 240 ticks instead of sinking to the terrain, with every existing aerodynamics check unchanged (rolls 541 units, liftoff at 6.04 units/tick, flaps 12 ticks earlier, stall sinks); `./bin/shank_server --level var/zombie/nextown_zombies.json` logs all six `[VEHICLE]` spawns at the deck coordinates (`scene=9`); `./bin/leveltool check` round-trips the level without dropping anything. NOT verified, named: the live "nextown" level arrives from the zombies registry when online, so a connected run serves whatever that copy holds until it is re-exported with the same `vehicle_spawns` (the checked-in file is the offline fallback and the reference layout); no windowed drive or takeoff was taken here, and an oriented/ramped box's floor is still its authored AABB.

- **fix(build): every warning the Windows release log carried is gone, and `make check-windows` now audits the LINK, not just the compile.** The previous fix repaired the two failures that actually stopped the link; this pass takes the rest of that log seriously, because three of the warnings were real bugs rather than noise. **`vehicle_burn_tick(vb, &b->health)`** passed an `int *` to a `float *` — undefined behaviour that wrote float bits into the authoritative hull, so the burn ladder that the vehicle-burn entry says "destroys the car" could never have drained a buggy correctly, and the smoke/fire thresholds read garbage. The ladder stays float (its own test, thresholds and PARENA curve are float), and `VehicleBurn` now owns the hull it is draining: the host seeds it from `b->health` when the burn is reset, re-seeds it whenever something else changed the hull (a shot, a repair — which is also what lets a repaired car go out), ticks it, and writes whole points back. **Six implicit `reflux_host_*` declarations** in `packages/reflux/reflux_mod.c`: PARENA emitted the bodies without the host prototypes, so the Windows build compiled them as implicit `int()` functions — UB, and the reason the file's own two consumers (`mk/labstation.mk`, `mk/shadow_war.mk`) had been force-including the header. The generated TU now includes `reflux_mod_host.h` itself, with a note that a regeneration must keep the line. **`labst_reach_tenths`** had no declaration anywhere a caller could see (its PARENA-generated TU has no header), so it is now declared by `lab_station_host.h`. **The rest were declarations and types**: `draw_string`/`draw_scene` are forward-declared where the phone panel and the broadcast render call them (implicit prototypes against real signatures), `draw_phone_app` takes a non-const `Phone *` because it syncs the EMPIRE economy into the phone it is handed, the spray decal's `sendto` casts to `const char *` (winsock's signature; POSIX accepts it too), and `winsock2.h` is now included **before** `windows.h` — which is exactly what the mingw `#warning` in the log asked for, since `windows.h` drags in the incompatible old `winsock.h`. **`make check-windows` gained the missing half of the release job**: it already compiled every `LOBBY_SRC` file; it now runs those objects through `nm` and fails on (1) any symbol defined more than once — the `s_abs` class, where two generated PARENA modules both export a helper — and (2) any undefined symbol the objects and the mingw import libraries do not define — the `mat_reflection_strength_permille` class, where a file was simply missing from `LOBBY_SRC`. SDL/GL/Win32/CRT names and the release job's separately-built objects (`editor_widget_*`, `phone_browser_*`) are allowlisted, so the check is quiet on a clean tree and reads as the linker's own complaint when it is not. Verified: `make check-windows` — **105 sources compile, link symbols clean** (and the audit was proven against the real failure first: on the unfixed tree it reports `s_abs` and `mat_reflection_strength_permille`); `make test-vehicle-burn` still passes with the ladder fed by the int hull; `make server` links; the lobby `main.c` compiles with **none** of the six warnings above. NOT verified, named: the Windows link itself still cannot be run here (SDL2 mingw headers are present, the `.a`/`.dll` kits and `../EDITOR.GAME` are not), so this is the object-level equivalent of the link, not a produced `ShankPit.exe`.

- **fix(build): the Windows release job links again — two source-level collisions it was the first build to see.** The job had compiled cleanly and then died at the link step with `multiple definition of 's_abs'` and `undefined reference to 'mat_reflection_strength_permille'`. Both are real, and neither could be caught by `make check-windows`, which deliberately stops after compiling. **`s_abs`**: `PARENA/stdlib/shankpit/slide_rules.prn` and `PARENA/stdlib/bedrockracers/web/br_stadium.prn` had each defined a private helper called `s-abs` (one for `I32`, one for `F64`). Generated functions are extern, and both modules are in `LOBBY_SRC`, so the two became one symbol with two bodies — a link error that only appears when the two files are linked together, which is exactly what the release build does and what the pre-existing compile-only check cannot. Fixed at the cause in the PARENA source rather than by renaming generated output: the slide module's helpers are now `slide-clamp`/`slide-abs`, matching the `slide_*` prefix of its own exported functions, and `packages/simulation/slide_rules.c` was regenerated with `make regen-slide-rules`. **`mat_reflection_strength_permille`**: the reflection work added `packages/simulation/material_rules.c` and its test, but never added the file to the Makefile's `LOBBY_SRC` — so `material_shaders.h`'s own call site had no definition in the client link, while the test target, which names the file explicitly, stayed green. `material_rules.c` is now in `LOBBY_SRC`, which is also the one source of truth CI expands (`make -s print-lobby-src`), so the release job picks it up with no hand-copy. Verified without a linker: every one of the 105 lobby sources was compiled for `x86_64-w64-mingw32` and the resulting objects were audited with `nm` — **0 duplicate global definitions** (was `s_abs`), `mat_reflection_strength_permille` and the renamed `slide_abs` each defined exactly once, and every symbol still undefined in the set is libc/Win32/SDL or `editor_widget_*` (which the release job links from its own EDITOR.GAME objects). Plus `make check-windows` (105 sources compile), `make test-slide-slope` **24 checks** and `make test-material-rules` green after the regeneration, and `make server` unaffected (`SERVER_SRC` never used either file's `mat_*`). NOT verified, named: the full Windows link itself was not run here — this box has the SDL2 mingw headers but not the `.a`/`.dll` libraries or `../EDITOR.GAME`, so the proof is the object-level audit that reproduces both reported failures' own cause, not a produced `ShankPit.exe`.

- **feat(empire): the shared world is now authored in the level, drawn in the HUD and replicated to allies — the three gaps the EMPIRE unit pass named.** Founder: *"this is almost like a level building affordance ... taking over territory gathering resources"*, and, for co-op, that an ally who joins the host is in the *same* world. **Authored**: the level schema gained `resource_nodes` (`x`, `z`, `amount`) and `claim_cells` (`x`, `z`, `radius`), parsed and re-serialized by `level_boxes.h` with the same small scanner every other array uses, capped at the world's own `EU_MAX_NODES`/`EU_MAX_CLAIM_CELLS`. Both hosts (`apps/server`'s `server_apply_custom_level`, the lobby's `lobby_apply_story_level`) unpack them into `story_ai_empire_load_level`, which falls back to the old default ring when a level authors neither key — so every pre-EMPIRE level is unchanged and an old level still has something to gather. Two schema decisions are honest rather than convenient: a node with `amount <= 0` is **skipped** (otherwise a unit walks to a dead spot and stands on it forever), and `radius 0` means "the host's own default", not a one-unit cell. **Drawn**: `eu_hud_text` (`empire_units.h`, pure, no GL) writes one line — units under orders, each faction's stockpile, ground held, nodes still worth walking to — and `draw_hud` draws it only when the process is actually ticking EMPIRE units, so nothing changes on a level with none. **Replicated**: new `PACKET_EMPIRE_STATE` (18) + `NetEmpireState` + `packages/simulation/empire_sync.h` (`fill`/`decode`/`apply`) send the **mutable** half at 1 Hz on the zone packet's own beat: stockpiles, each node's remaining amount, each cell's owner. Positions, radii and capacities are deliberately **not** on the wire — both sides loaded the same level (or generated the same zone from one seed), and a spoofed packet must not be able to move a resource node — while the counts travel so a client on an older world applies only the overlap. An unknown faction byte becomes neutral, a count past the struct's own arrays is refused outright, and a world with no EMPIRE units stays silent instead of broadcasting zeroes every second. Verified: `make test-empire-world` (the schema: parse, a skipped zero-amount node, an authored radius, absent keys empty, save -> load round trip), `make test-empire-sync` — **24 checks, 0 failed** (host world -> bytes -> decode -> apply mirrors stockpiles/amounts/ownership; wrong type, short datagram, oversized node and cell counts all refused; out-of-range owner lands neutral; nothing moves; overlap-only apply; an empty host still makes a valid packet), and `make test-empire-units` — now **59 checks, 0 failed** (adds `eu_world_load`'s own authored/capped/empty cases and the HUD line's contents). Plus `make test-empire-spawn` still 16/16, `make test-empire-rules` still green, and `make server` links with the broadcast and the level load in place. NOT verified, named: **no two-client session was driven**, so the replication is proven at the packet seam rather than observed between machines; **the HUD line is asserted as text, not seen on screen** (no window here); **NOCK has no placement UI for nodes or claim cells yet**, so authoring them means writing the level JSON (the schema, loader, serializer and both hosts are done — the editor affordance is the remaining piece); and `make lobby` still cannot link on this box because `../EDITOR.GAME` is not checked out, so the client half is a clean `-fsyntax-only` compile, not a run.

- **feat(empire): units you can spawn — citizen / zombie / soldier / ally / guard / worker — with a human-vs-beast inheritance and programmable orders, plus the Windows build fix the first attempt exposed.** Founder: *"spawn in allies like mike etc and they need their own ais etc for exploring the environment engaging enemies following allies taking over territory gathering resources ... the ais also need some generic stuff that all humans will inherit but not all ais will be human ... spawn in citizens zombies soldiers etc"*. Two EMPIRE modules now exist as siblings, deliberately not one file: the influence economy + 72-district sphere (`empire_rules.prn/.c`, already there) and this **unit model** (`PARENA/stdlib/shankpit/empire_units.prn` → `packages/simulation/empire_units_rules.c` by `make regen-empire-units`). An **archetype** is a complete spawn: brain role, default skin, faction, accepted orders and health — ally defaults to **Mike** because the founder named him, and an authored skin always wins, so an imported NOCK skin is a legal ally body. **Inheritance** is a switch, not a column: every human archetype inherits one baseline row (courage 600 / aggression 500 / loyalty 700 / greed 500 / curiosity 500) and the zombie inherits the beast row (900/900/0/0/300) — a zombie is not a human with different numbers, it has no loyalty or greed to spend. **Orders** are the programmable half: a `EU_ORDER_*` mask (follow / explore / engage / gather / claim / guard) and per-archetype priorities, all in PARENA, where the mask is the *only* gate — every archetype scores every order, so "a citizen that fights" is a mask with `engage` in it, not new C. The decision engages first when an enemy is visible, otherwise takes the highest-priority triggered order with `guard` as the standing fallback, and returns **none** to mean "use the role's own brain" rather than freezing a unit. It is carried out by three thin runs (`AI_MODE_EXPLORE/GATHER/CLAIM`) over the same movement primitive every other mode uses, and a per-process `EUWorld` (resource nodes, 30-second claim cells, per-faction stockpiles) that `story_ai_reset` seeds with a default contested ring. **Authoring**: `characters[]` gained `archetype`/`faction`/`orders` (parsed and re-serialized by `level_boxes.h`, written only for `archetype != 0` so old levels stay byte-identical), both spawn call sites route `archetype > 0` through `story_ai_spawn_unit`, and NOCK's character inspector gained Archetype/Faction/Orders dropdowns. `docs/EMPIRE_UNITS.md` is the contract. **The Windows build fix is real and was mine**: renaming this module to avoid the existing `empire_rules` files left `empire_rules.h` deleted, and that header is what declares the influence API the phone calls — plus my new module had reused `g_empire_rules`/`empire_rules_install`. The lobby now includes `empire_rules_host.h` and `lab_station_host.h` explicitly (killing the `empire_*` and `labst_reach_tenths` implicit declarations in the CI log), and the unit module uses `EUUnitRules`/`g_eu_rules`/`eu_rules_install`. Verified: `make test-empire-spawn` — **16 checks, 0 failed** on the wiring the unit test cannot reach: an archetype becomes a real player slot with its role, Mike's skin and its own health pool, an authored skin/faction/mask wins, the order really moves from `follow` to `gather` as the world changes, a node is drained through the real gather run, thirty seconds on a cell really flips its owner, and a zombie with nothing in sight gets no order and falls back to its brain. That test found **two real bugs**: `story_ai_tick` returned early outside `MODE_STORY`/`MODE_TYLER`, so a level-authored unit in ZOMBIES or a custom map would have spawned and stood still forever (a live EMPIRE unit now makes the tick legal on its own, archetype-0 characters keep the old gate), and ally perception treated the hero as an enemy — `ai_gather_perception`'s only target is `players[0]`, so an ally's first look at the player it follows would have picked `engage` (`enemy_visible` is now forced off for the ally faction). It also runs in `MODE_ZOMBIES` on purpose so the gate widening is pinned by a test, not a comment. Plus `make test-empire-units` — **50 checks, 0 failed** (PARENA == the header defaults across every archetype field, order priority, both inheritance rows and the world numbers; every human archetype inherits the human row and the zombie does not; an authored mask replaces the archetype's and its bits are usable; engage-outranks-everything, follow-a-distant-leader, gather, explore-when-idle, claim, guard-as-fallback and none-when-nothing-applies; a node yields exactly its amount; a claim takes exactly 30 s and a rival starts its own clock), `make test-empire-rules` still green, `make server` links, the lobby compiles clean, and **`make check-windows` (mingw) passes**. NOT done, named: no live match was played, so no ally has been watched walking to a node; nodes/cells are **not level-authored** (the default ring is all there is) and nothing draws the stockpiles; claims/stockpiles are per-host and do not replicate; and the NOCK editor change is type-reviewed only (no `node_modules` here), while the full Windows CI command also needs `EDITOR.GAME` and the SDL2 mingw kit, neither in this checkout.

- **feat(skins): an imported set is now a skin you can pick — for yourself and for an NPC — and the loader that was supposed to do that had never once succeeded.** Founder: *"go from nock importer into importing it as a skin so i can select it from skins menu and then it needs to work as my skin also i need to be able to import additional skins to use for npcs etc"*. Closing that exposed a real, total bug: `gband_skel_npc_load_clipset` resolved the mesh directory by stripping only the set's own name, leaving `.../clipsets` — where no `.gskel` exists — so **every** import failed at `gskel_init` while looking perfectly complete on disk. The mount rule is the opposite of what the code assumed: a set sits at `<assets>/clipsets/<name>/` and its mesh is the *grandparent* (`assets/goldenband`), so `skin.json`'s own `"assets"` field is now **checked against** that path instead of appended to it, with a loud stderr line when they disagree. Second real constraint: the kit table held **8** slots and six were already taken by the fixed kits, so "import additional skins" would have run out after two — `GBAND_SKEL_NPC_MAX_KITS` is 16. The chain itself is deliberately not a parallel system: `skin_registry.h` scans the real tree (a set needs `skin.json`, `anim_idle` and `anim_walk`; a directory without a mesh is **refused**, because channels only mean something against one rig), the **SKINS menu** lists the built-ins then one row per imported set, and choosing one pins `SKIN_MANNEQUIN` as the draw path while the set index says which rig — saved as **two** numbers in `shankpit_skin.cfg`, so an old one-number config still loads. For NPCs, `AIKit` grew `AI_KIT_IMPORTED_BASE` (`story_ai.h`), an authored `LevelCharacter.kit` of base+index picks an imported body, and the host **prints each set's own authored number** at startup so a designer reads it off the log instead of guessing. The two hand-written copies of `kit >= AI_KIT_AUTO && kit <= AI_KIT_GEORGE` in `apps/server` and `apps/lobby` became one `story_ai_kit_is_valid`, because a range that has grown is exactly how an imported skin silently stops being authorable; a value past what this build loaded falls through to the role/round-robin cascade rather than drawing nothing. Verified: `make test-skin-registry` — **18 checks, 0 failed**: the committed Mike set is discovered by name, its `skin.json` binds the mesh, all thirteen contract clips are present, the bundle loads as a real kit with a skeleton (the ragdoll pool needs one), and a **counting draw callback sees vertices actually skinned** (no GL context needed); a **second** set in the real `<assets>/clipsets/<name>` layout is offered under its own name and takes its **own kit slot**, not the first one's; a missing directory is zero offers rather than an error; a set with no `skin.json` is skipped and refused by the loader directly. Plus `make test-clipset-import` **22/22** and `make test-local-nock` **16/16** (the import and level contracts are untouched), and the whole lobby `main.c` **compiles clean** (`gcc -fsyntax-only`, SDL2 headers installed here for exactly this check). NOT done, named: **`make lobby` still cannot link on this box** because `../EDITOR.GAME/gen/` is not checked out, so the check is a compile, not a run — no screenshot, no menu clicked, no body seen; an imported skin plays **four of the thirteen clips** (idle, walk, melee-as-greet, ability-as-dance) through the NPC kit path, not the full state machine; **a client's own imported choice does not replicate** — a coop peer sees the server's `forced_kit` or the usual heuristic, because there is no wire field for "this player's selected set"; and the NOCK **editor dropdown does not yet list imported sets** for authoring, so an NPC's kit number is still typed by hand from the startup log.

- **feat(nock): importing a Mixamo clip set and committing it is now a thing you do in the editor, not a command you remember.** `tools/local_nock.py` gained `GET/POST /api/clipsets` and `tools/nock_editor.html` gained a **Character clips** panel: one dropdown per contract state, auto-map by clip name (`UAL1_Standard_Walk_Loop`, `mixamo_run`), and **Import + commit** — which writes `assets/goldenband/clipsets/<name>/` and commits it with a message naming the set. It is the *same* `tools/mixamo_clipset.py` binder the CLI runs, imported rather than reimplemented, so the editor and the command cannot disagree about what a valid bundle is; refusals arrive as the binder's own sentences ("no clip for: anim_run, anim_ability", "content_hash mismatch…") because the artist is who has to act on them. Only clips with **both** `.gband` and `.gband.json` are offered: the binary is channel-blind, so a lone binary would only produce a failed import. The **licensing boundary is a design decision, not a footnote**: Adobe's terms allow baking Mixamo into a game but not redistributing the editable source, so nothing here ingests or stores a raw `.fbx` — the import path starts from baked `.gband` clips this repo already owns, and a staging folder for raw downloads stays personal and gitignored. `docs/MIXAMO_PIPELINE.md` records the boundary, the three steps (convert → bind → play) and the IDUNA gap. Verified: `make test-clipset-import` — **22 checks, 0 failed** against a real HTTP server over a throwaway git repo holding copies of Mike's real clips: the contract and package path come back as the panel needs them, a lone binary is not offered, an incomplete rig is **refused until allow-partial**, partial binds exactly the two states the names match and **commits them** (with the set named in `git log`), an explicit 13-state mapping binds all thirteen and warns that reusing a clip is reusing a clip, a path-shaped name is refused, an empty import is refused, a clip whose data no longer matches its hash is refused **with no half-bundle left behind**, and re-importing identical content reports "no changes to commit" instead of an empty commit. Plus the existing `make test-local-nock` — still **16/16**, so the level/live-session contract it shares the server with is untouched. NOT done, named: **IDUNA's cloud endpoint is not built** — there is no Go toolchain on the box this was developed on, so instead of shipping an unverified handler into a large service, `docs/MIXAMO_PIPELINE.md` specifies the exact `POST /admin/nock/api/shankpit-clipsets` contract the local endpoint already implements (validation, `{bound, missing, warnings}`, store-not-git in the cloud path); IDUNA's glTF → GOLDENBAND conversion itself already exists. And nothing *draws* from these bundles yet — that is still the renderer slice.

- **feat(vehicles): a burning car now actually burns — the fire ladder moved onto the authoritative hull, so it destroys the car and hurts the people standing next to it.** The previous vehicle-burn entry shipped the ladder and named this exact gap: *"the hull and the blast are the local/client sim's — there is no wire field for a burning car yet, so a networked match does not replicate the fire state"*. The honest reading turned out better than a new wire field: the fire is a pure function of **hull**, the hull is already replicated (`NetBuggy.health`), and both halves of the ladder are deterministic — so the missing piece was never a packet, it was that nothing on the server ever ran it. `buggy_tick_all` (`packages/simulation/local_game.h`, the same function both the server and the lobby's local sim use) now ticks `vehicle_burn_tick` against `b->health` **in place**, so smoke at 150 and fire at 75 happen to the authoritative car: the hull genuinely drains 0.25/tick once alight, the fuse reaches the tank at 300 burning ticks, and the **detonation is `explode_splash` on the authority** with PARENA's own `blast_damage`/`blast_radius_milli`, which means allies see a car that is dying rather than one that merely looks like it is, and a driver who parks a burning car next to a teammate damages the teammate. The lobby keeps its own ladder for the *look* (BR's fx-fire-rise/fall flame, the flickering light, the smoke), still driven from the replicated health — now falling on its own, because the server is the one taking it down. Seeded from the car's own id so fuse and flicker are that car's on every host, and reset when a car is replaced rather than inheriting a dead one's burn. `buggy_tick_all` gained the clock (`now_ms`: `get_server_time()` on the server, `cmd_time` in `local_update`) because a blast needs a timestamp for hit feedback; the header's forward prototype moved with it — the first build failed with a real `conflicting types for 'buggy_tick_all'`, which is exactly what that prototype block exists to catch. Verified: `make -B server` links (the authority compiles the new path), `make test-vehicle-burn` still passes every check, the whole client `main.c` cross-compiles for mingw (so the lobby's own `local_game.h` path is unchanged in shape), and a live headless `./bin/shank_server --dungeon` boot generated its zone and ran without the burn tick faulting. NOT verified, named: **no live two-client burn was driven** — there is no scripted way here to crash a car to 75 hp on a server with an occupant, so the claim that allies see it is reasoning from the replicated health field plus the shared deterministic ladder, not an observed session; smoke is still modelled and not drawn as its own plume; and the C-110's cargo ramp, airborne spawn and cargo drops remain unbuilt.

- **feat(anim): the Mixamo half is finished — a binder that produces the thirteen-clip bundle, and a selector that loads and plays it.** The previous anim entry ended by naming exactly this gap: *"no .gband-from-Mixamo importer in this repo, the kit loader still takes four clip names, and nothing is drawn"*. The shipped assets are real Mixamo-derived clips named after their source (`UAL1_Standard_Idle_Loop`, `Mike_Idle`, ...), and the engine loads by contract name, so the missing piece was never a DCC importer — it was the two ends. **Binder** (`tools/mixamo_clipset.py`): given a directory of real `.gband` + `.gband.json` pairs and either a `--map` JSON or `--auto` keyword resolution, it writes `<out>/anim_idle.gband(+.json)` … `anim_climb_loop`, preserving the channel order verbatim (it *is* the data layout), the content hash, the tick rate and the duration, and owning only the contract fields: state name, index, source clip, and loop metadata that mirrors `anim_rules.prn` (postures loop over their full duration; actions are one-shot with `end_tick` 0). It refuses, by name, the failures that would otherwise be a silently wrong animation: a missing state, a binary that is not `GBND`/v1 or whose length disagrees with its header, a manifest whose `tick_rate`/`duration_ticks`/`channels` disagree with the binary, **channel data that no longer matches its own `content_hash`** (a clip edited on disk without rehashing), and **clips from two different `skeleton_hash`es** — the one error the engine could never see, because channels are matched by name and a shoulder from another rig is still spelled `Shoulder.L.qx`. Ambiguous `--auto` candidates are an error listing them, never a coin flip. **Selector** (`packages/simulation/anim_clipset.h`, header-only, no GL/SDL): loads all thirteen through `gseq_clip_load`, ticks the chosen clip at its own `tick_rate`, wraps looping states by duration, **holds a one-shot's last tick** (a reload snapping back to frame 0 reads as a glitch), starts the rules' own crossfade on entry, and falls back — run → idle → whatever loaded — so a rig with four clips animates instead of drawing nothing, while `anim_clipset_tick` returns -1 when nothing loaded so a caller knows not to draw a pose at all. Verified: `make test-mixamo-clipset` — **17 checks** over real Mike/Leela assets: auto-mapping a 13-clip rig, all outputs contract-named, channels and hashes preserved, posture/action loop metadata, a missing state refused by name, `--allow-partial`, tampered data caught by hash, two rigs refused, a truncated binary refused, ambiguity refused, `--dry-run` writing nothing, and a shared clip warning instead of failing. `make test-anim-clipset` — **34 checks** through GOLDENBAND's real loader: thirteen clips load, channels and rates come from the manifest, a posture wraps rather than holds, RUN/FIRE/MELEE are selected from real `PlayerState` fields with FIRE marked additive and MELEE outranking it, entering a state restarts at tick 0 and begins the crossfade, a one-shot holds `duration-1`, a crouch owns the pose, a one-clip bundle plays idle for everything and still yields a pose, and an empty bundle returns -1/NULL rather than garbage. Two real problems that run caught: the selector resolved the **previous** `state` instead of the wanted one (so every test stayed IDLE — a genuine bug, not a test artifact), and my first assertion used a 16 ms step against 30 Hz clips, which is zero whole ticks. NOT done, named: **nothing draws from the clip set yet** — the player body is still `compute_player_anim_pose`, the NPC kit loader still takes idle/walk/greet/dance, and the sampled channels have not been run through forward kinematics into a skinned draw; that is a renderer slice and this box has no GPU or display to verify one.

- **feat(stats): GFD's 23 jobs finally reach this engine as numbers — table copied from its own source, mapping ours, in PARENA.** Phase 4 of `docs/GFD_UNIFICATION.md`. The founder: *"the stat progression stuff can all be tied together"*. Read before writing: GFD is checked out at `../GoblinFoxDragon`, and its job model is `server/job/job.go` — 23 real jobs in `AllJobs` order (**including GFD's own non-canonical 23rd, ASN**), each with base HP/MP, per-level curves, and seven primaries. That table is copied **verbatim** into `PARENA/stdlib/shankpit/stats_bridge.prn` (generated to `packages/simulation/stats_bridge.c` by `scripts/gen_stats_bridge.sh` / `make regen-stats-bridge`), because retuning a job is GFD's decision and this file must not quietly invent a different Warrior. Everything **derived** is this engine's, in I32 permille, because GFD resolves damage against a mob registry and a dice model that do not exist here: haste as a **multiplier on a weapon's own ROF ticks** (never a rewrite of it — a knife stays a knife), a damage **scalar** on `WPN_STATS`, movement narrow enough (850..1150) that the vehicles and terrain tuning still read right, crit off DEX, evade off AGI+level capped at 35% because a 60 Hz shooter cannot sell invisible hits, and health clamped to **255** because `NetPlayer.health` is an `unsigned char` and capping beats wrapping to zero. The boundary the plan asked for is kept: `packages/simulation/stats_bridge.h` is a flat `StatsBridge` of function pointers over plain ints, so **no C file learns about 23 jobs**, and it carries identical C defaults so any binary or test can use the bridge without linking generated code (the same contract `zone_rules.h`/`widget_rules.h`/`slide_rules.h` keep). Two behaviours are total on purpose: a negative job id reads as WAR, an id past the table as ASN (different cases, different meaning), and levels clamp to 1..75. Verified: `make test-stats-bridge` — **40 checks, 0 failed**: every one of the 23 jobs' eight base values matches an **independent transcription of `job.go`** (so a slip in either place fails instead of shipping a nerfed Warrior); the id order is GFD's own; `NIN` resolves to 12 while `nin` and `QQQ` do not; the generated rules and the header agree on **every derived number for all 23 jobs at levels −2..80** and on cooldown across ROF 0..60 x haste 600..1400; GFD's level curve is exact (`WAR` 90 at level 1, 105 at 2, linear to 75, flat past it); melee jobs have **no MP at any level** (job.go's own rule) while casters grow per level; a level-1 BLM is squishier than a WAR and a level-75 anything caps at 255 rather than wrapping; `NIN` has more haste and evade than `WAR` without exceeding the caps; a 20-tick knife ROF never becomes 0 or a 999-tick monster; and an out-of-range id is a real lookup, not a read past the table. That run also caught a genuine inconsistency in **my own** contract: the name helper clamped an unknown id to ASN while its doc promised WAR — fixed in the helper, and the rules' comment now states the real (and deliberately asymmetric) fallback. NOT done, named: **nothing applies a job to a live match yet** — no call site reads the bridge, so a player is still 100 HP and the stock ROF whatever a save says; equipment and elemental affinities are untouched (they need GFD's item model); nothing persists; and the dungeon's `difficulty` tier is generated but not yet fed into a stat curve.

- **feat(zones): a world announces itself on the LAN, so an ally runs `--coop-find` instead of being told an IP.** The last named gap in phase 3: `--coop-host` made hosting one command, but the joiner still needed an address typed at them. New `packages/simulation/zone_beacon.{h,c}`: a **fixed 48-byte** beacon (magic, version, game port, players, seed, NUL-padded zone id), announced by the server on the **same 1 Hz beat as `PACKET_ZONE_STATE`** to the broadcast address **and to loopback** — the broadcast is the LAN's copy, and the loopback copy is what lets the host's own finder see its world and what makes the discovery test run on a box with no broadcast route. It carries **identity only**, exactly like the zone packet: a joiner still generates the dungeon locally from the seed, so nothing about the geometry is transferred and the beacon stays 48 bytes forever. `zone_beacon_find` binds the beacon port, waits with a 100 ms `select` loop, and drops anything that is not a valid beacon (that port is open to every process on the LAN) — and the address it returns is the **datagram's source**, never a field inside the packet, so a forged beacon can only point a player at a host that will not answer. The id rule is shared with `--coop-host` (`coop_host_zone_valid`) rather than restated, because the decodable id is still a filename on the receiving side (`var/maps/proc_<id>.json`). Server: `--beacon-port N` (0 disables advertising), announced only while the zone is live, and it advertises `g_server_game_port` — the port this process actually bound, not the constant 6969, since a beacon pointing at 6969 while the world runs elsewhere sends allies to nothing. Client: `--coop-find [beacon-port]` listens, then joins the same `MODE_QUEUE` path the host uses. Verified: `make test-zone-beacon` — **33 checks, 0 failed**: encode/decode round-trips zone, seed, players and port; reserved bytes stay zero; and the refusals are the real work — a short, over-long, NULL or wrong-magic datagram, a future version, an empty id, an **unterminated** id (no NUL inside its field), an id that is a path (`../etc`), an id that looks like a flag (`-rf`), game port 0 and a privileged port are all refused; a too-small encode buffer and an invalid zone/port are never advertised; a negative player count clamps to 0 instead of wrapping into the packet. Then the live half: the test starts the actual server on `--port 7351 --beacon-port 7352`, waits for it to write its zone, calls the same `zone_beacon_find` the client calls, and asserts it heard the world **named the one that is running**, with **the real game port**, a sane player count, the seed, and a source address that parses as IPv4. `make test-coop-host` still 40/40 (the server source list gained `zone_beacon.c`/`coop_host.c`), and the whole client `main.c` (with `--coop-find` wired) cross-compiles for mingw with no errors. NOT verified, named: **the client's own find-and-connect was compiled, not run** — `make lobby` cannot link here (`../EDITOR.GAME` absent, no native SDL2 headers) and there is no display, so the live proof is the server-side beacon plus the finder, not a joiner's window. Discovery is also **same-broadcast-domain only** (no relay, no internet lobby), and the beacon says nothing about map identity beyond the zone id and seed — a host running a hand-authored `--level` advertises only its zone.

- **feat(zones): hosting a LAN world is one command — `--coop-host` starts the server and joins it.** Phase 3 of `docs/GFD_UNIFICATION.md`, and the piece the previous entry named as missing. `./bin/shank_lobby --coop-host [zone]` now spawns the real `bin/shank_server` for that zone as a child process (`packages/simulation/coop_host.c`, same fork/exec and `CreateProcessA(CREATE_NO_WINDOW)` pattern `lobby_launch_nock` already uses at `--port 8787`), points `SERVER_HOST` at loopback, and enters **`MODE_QUEUE`** through the exact `reset_client_render_state_for_net(); net_connect();` path the menu's TDM tile uses — so the host is a networked player, not a local special case, and an ally on the LAN joins the host's world with `--host <host-lan-ip>` and the same netcode. Deliberately not the existing `--dungeon` flag: that builds the zone in-process for solo play and has no server anyone can reach, which is why it could never be co-op. Two decisions in there are load-bearing rather than convenience. **The UDP port probe**: a UDP port has no `accept`, so `coop_host_port_busy` binds it briefly and lets go — which is what makes relaunching **attach** to a world that is still up instead of spawning a second server that cannot bind and dies, and what makes quitting-and-rejoining the same dungeon work at all. **Zone ids are validated at the flag** (`[A-Za-z0-9_-]`, never starting with `-`) because the id is a real filename component on both sides (`var/maps/proc_<zone>.json`) *and* an argv element, which is two injection surfaces for one unchecked string. A failure never writes `SERVER_HOST`/`SERVER_PORT`, so a missing binary or a refused bind leaves the player in the lobby with the reason on screen (`app_launch_status`) instead of silently connecting to the internet default. Verified: `make test-coop-host` — **40 checks, 0 failed**: defaults are the server's own defaults; a zone with a slash, `..`, a leading `-`, a space, a dot or a backslash is refused, as are a privileged and an out-of-range port; the child argv is exactly `{ bin, --port, 6969, --dungeon, <zone>, NULL }` with the port as a decimal string; the port probe reads free on an unused port, **busy against a socket the test really binds**, and free again after close; and then the live part — the test calls the same `coop_host_start` the lobby calls against the actual `bin/shank_server`, gets a pid, **waits for `var/maps/proc_coop_selftest.json` to appear** (the server's own output, not the fork's return code), confirms the port is held, confirms a **second start attaches instead of spawning a twin**, stops it, and confirms the port is released. `coop_host.c` also cross-compiles clean for mingw (`x86_64-w64-mingw32-gcc -Wall -Wextra -Werror`), which is the branch that runs on Windows. NOT done, named: **no LAN broadcast/discovery** — an ally still types the host's IP, nothing announces the world; **the server outlives the lobby** (a child process, so quitting the host leaves the world up until it is killed — deliberate for now, but a policy that needs a decision); **no in-process host**; and the client's own `--coop-host` wiring is **reviewed, not compiled on this box** — `make lobby` cannot link here at all because `../EDITOR.GAME` is not checked out and there are no native SDL2 headers, so `main.c` could only be compiled for mingw, which already fails on pre-existing `empire_*` implicit declarations unrelated to this change.

- **feat(zones): a LAN client joins the SAME procedural dungeon from its seed, not from a level transfer.** Founder: *"the first person to enter it it generates and then everyone else enters the same one"*. The server now hosts a zone: `./bin/shank_server --dungeon [zone]` generates it once, applies it through the ordinary custom-level path, and broadcasts **`PACKET_ZONE_STATE`** (`packages/common/protocol.h`, wired at 1 Hz beside the snapshot) carrying the zone id, its seed, occupancy, the registry's state and how long it has been empty. No boxes cross the wire: a client decodes the packet with the new `packages/simulation/zone_sync.h` and **rebuilds the identical layout from the seed** with the same `proc_dungeon.h` the host used, which is only sound because generation is deterministic (`make test-proc-dungeon` proves one seed writes byte-identical JSON). The reload rule is the real work: `zone_sync_should_reload` returns true only when the zone id or the seed changed, so a repeated 1 Hz packet is a no-op instead of rebuilding the world — and with it every spray, decal and cached shot — every second. Occupancy on the server is the connected player count: the first client in generates, the last one out starts the fifteen-minute countdown, expiry deletes the layout file and clears the seed, and the next arrival regenerates from a **new** seed, which is exactly what makes the clients reload. An idle zone with the same seed does not reload (it is still the same dungeon).
